Title: Lower Bounds For Gradient-Free Convex Optimization And Convex-Concave Saddle-Point Problems

Published Time: Fri, 02 Oct 2026 00:13:07 GMT

Number of Pages: 94

Markdown Content:
# Lower Bounds For Gradient-Free Convex Optimization And Convex-Concave Saddle-Point Problems 

Yuriy Dorn 1, Darina Dvinskikh 2, Timofei Loginov 3,

Osman Osmanov 4, Aleksandr Shestakov 5,

Nazarii Tupitsa 5, Alexander Gasnikov 41 AI Institute MSU (msu.ru)  

> 2

HSE University (hse.ru)  

> 3

Moscow Independent Research Institute of Artificial Intelligence (miriai.org)  

> 4

Innopolis University (innopolis.university)  

> 5

Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence (mbzuai.ac.ae) 

Abstract 

We study the query complexity of optimization with exact scalar-value information. For globally L-smooth convex functions on Rd with a minimizer in a Euclidean ball of radius R,we prove the lower bound Ω( d min {d, pLR 2/ε }) for adaptive randomized algorithms in the stated accuracy range. Queries are unrestricted, and the bound contains no logarithmic factor. It holds for quadratics and matches the minimum of two elementary upper bounds on that class. Prescribed strong convexity introduces the additional cap pL/μ . The proof compares the mean of a matrix with the mean of its inverse on arbitrary affine slices and bounds posterior moments after adaptive measurements. We extend these information bounds to saddle-point problems with independent block dimensions. For compact bilinear games, the coupling alone yields Ω( r min {r, pCR xRy /ε }) queries, where r = min {dx, d y }. A strongly convex–strongly concave construction yields a condition-dependent lower bound even with known isotropic diagonal Hessians. Separate reductions establish scalar query lower bounds under mixed value and partial-gradient access, including for outer objective accuracy. A Euclidean-image radius yields smooth and nonsmooth extensions to independently specified norms and known penalties. 

# 1 Introduction 

Gradient-free optimization minimizes an objective using function values. Query complexity counts the scalar evaluations required for a prescribed objective error. For convex problems, we seek its joint dependence on dimension, accuracy, and geometry. Finite differences and randomized directional estimates connect value queries to first-order methods by approximating gradients from nearby evaluations (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017). Non-Euclidean proximal methods can improve the dimension dependence of these upper bounds. Under Euclidean regularity, the accelerated method of Gorbunov et al. (2022) has a √d dimension factor with an ℓ1 setup, while the nonsmooth two-point method of Shamir (2017) has only logarithmic dimension dependence. Establishing their optimality requires lower bounds covering all adaptive value-query methods under the same regularity and localization assumptions. Earlier lower bounds address complementary information models. Yao (1975) studied determin-istic exact quadratic recovery, and Protasov (1996) studied approximate quadratic minimization from values. Stochastic and noisy observations yield norm-dependent or noise-dependent lower 1arXiv:2610.00345v1 [math.OC] 29 Sep 2026 bounds (Duchi et al., 2015; Yu et al., 2023, 2024). For exact values of a fixed objective, Zhang et al. (2026); Kerger (2026) establish nonsmooth convex lower bounds. Here we study smooth and nonsmooth exact-value complexity with independent regularity and localization geometries. We allow arbitrary finite queries, free computation and precision, and adaptive randomized algorithms with a fixed worst-case budget and success probability at least 2/3 on every objective. 

Contributions. Dimension and accuracy. We prove d min {d, pLR 2/ε }/4096 , matching quadratic complexity and full-class complexity at moderate accuracy (Theorem 2.1 and Corollar-ies 2.2 and 2.3). A distinct multiscale construction gives a dimension-times-logarithm obstruction and matching full-class bounds uniform for μ/L ∈ [1 /2, 1] (Theorems 2.5 and E.5). 

Tight non-Euclidean gradient-free lower bounds. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ 2, q = p/ (p − 1) , with q = ∞

at p = 1 , and let Rp be the ℓp radius. For globally convex objectives, Euclidean L2-smoothness with a minimizer in this ball and Euclidean M2-Lipschitz continuity with minimization on it give, respectively,

To our knowledge, these are the first lower bounds for unrestricted randomized exact-value algorithms matching both rates for 1 ≤ p < 2, up to logarithmic factors in the windows of Corollaries 6.1 and 6.2. The nonsmooth result transfers the Euclidean information bound of Zhang et al. (2026). 

Arbitrary norms and known regularizers. The Euclidean-image profile Rm(B, K ) gives lower bounds with independent regularity and localization geometries (Theorems 3.1 and 3.3). Actual regularizer sublevels can improve the lower-bound formula by an unbounded factor (Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.4). 

Saddle points and information. We isolate coupling and mixed-oracle costs for unequal block dimensions (Section 5). The smooth information argument uses an affine-slice inverse comparison and an adaptive posterior trace identity (Theorem A.1 and Section 4). Tables 1 and 2 summarize the minimization and saddle-point bounds; Sections H and N give the detailed comparisons. 

# 2 Problem setting and the Euclidean lower bound 

Throughout, ∥ · ∥ 2 denotes the Euclidean norm, Bd

> 2

(R) = {x ∈ Rd : ∥x∥2 ≤ R} the closed Euclidean ball, and argmin f the set of global minimizers of f . Let Cd,L,R be the class of globally 

L-smooth convex functions with a global minimizer in the ball of radius R, that is, of convex differentiable functions f : Rd → R satisfying 

∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥2 ≤ L∥x − y∥2 for all x, y, argmin f ∩ Bd

> 2

(R)̸ = ∅.

Regularity and localization are measured in the same Euclidean norm here; Section 3 allows the two norms to differ. The oracle returns the exact value f (x) at any queried point x ∈ Rd. An algorithm is equipped with an independent random seed on a standard Borel probability space, and its query and output maps are total Borel functions of that seed and of the finite history of real-valued answers. Queries may be adaptive, their norms and precision are unrestricted, and internal computation is free. A deterministic budget T bounds the number of queries on every history; a run that stops early is padded with queries at zero. An algorithm is successful on a class if 

f (bx) − min f ≤ ε with probability at least 2/3

# 3 Non-Euclidean smooth and nonsmooth lower bounds 

Let B be the unit ball of a norm ∥ · ∥ with dual norm ∥ · ∥ ∗, and let K ⊂ Rd be nonempty, compact, and convex. The norm controlling regularity and the set localizing a solution are independent. For a known continuous convex penalty ψ : K → R, normalize min K ψ = 0 and write F = g + λψ , λ ≥ 0. Values and proximal computations involving ψ are free. 3.1 Spectral dependence of known regularization 

A scalar upper bound on the curvature of a known regularizer can lose information relevant to the oracle lower bound. The following specialization of Theorem 3.2 keeps the whole spectrum. The oracle still reveals only exact values of the unknown smooth part g; the penalty and its proximal operations are available for free, and the output must be in K.

Why the spectrum matters. For H = diag( In, AI n), the choice m = n retains n directions at regularization cost λR 2/2, independently of the larger curvature A. Replacing H by the scalar majorant AI charges the larger cost in every direction. This distinction persists after optimizing both the image dimension and the sublevel. Sharp complexity for general regularizers remains open. 

# 4 Why adaptive exact queries still have a cost 

For the quadratic construction, one function value reveals one rank-one linear measurement of a Wishart matrix W . Conditional on the seed and the complete adaptive transcript, its law is the original density restricted to the realized affine slice. This calculation applies to total Borel query policies. The sharp comparison in Theorem A.1 gives EW −1 ⪯ (1 + ( d + 1) /(2 ζ))( EW )−1

on every such slice. Combined with an exact trace identity, it bounds the expected operator norm of the posterior inverse in terms of the number of observations (Equations (21) and (22)). Successful optimization would produce a sufficiently accurate estimate of W −1u; the posterior budget then forces the dimension–accuracy product. The normalization is supported on valid positive-definite quadratics with probability at least 

49 /50 . Three independent runs, each followed by one validation query, give the correlation needed for the lower bound. All 3( T + 1) queries are counted. A separate spherical family gives the baseline 5d/ 16 and covers the small-parameter regime. The analytic comparison, conditioning argument, and calibration are detailed in Sections A and B. The logarithmic result uses different instances: nested smooth perturbations with independent spherical directions. Outside a selected child ball, deeper perturbations vanish exactly. Entering a child requires recovering its hidden direction, and a single query may enter several children. The proof in Section E accounts for these cascades to obtain the dimension-times-depth lower bound.

# 6 Matching non-Euclidean gradient-free rates 

The geometric lower bounds match known gradient-free upper bounds in two classes. Fix 

1 ≤ p ≤ 2 and q = p/ (p − 1) , with q = ∞ at p = 1 . In each statement, Tval

Dimension dependence and accuracy regimes. At p = 1 , the smooth rate has a √d factor, whereas the nonsmooth rate has no polynomial dimension factor. At p = 2 , both have a d factor. These statements concern ℓp localization or feasibility under Euclidean regularity; replacing 

L2, M 2 by Lp, M p changes the class. At the lower endpoints of (16) and (18), the respective lower bounds reach their dimension-dependent caps. Each two-point observation costs two scalar values. Running an expected-error upper guarantee to ε/ 3 gives success at least 2/3 by Markov’s inequality with a budget fixed in advance. 

6.1 Available upper bounds for arbitrary norms 

Known gradient-free algorithms yield upper bounds for every finite-dimensional regularity norm. For its unit ball B and a known nonempty compact convex set K, write

The smooth class has a global minimizer in K and permits unrestricted output. The nonsmooth class is minimized over K and requires a feasible output; the average of the mirror-descent iterates has this property. Both objectives are globally defined, so the finite-difference queries outside K are admissible. A singleton K needs no oracle calls. 

Proximal geometry. Sharper bounds retain the proximal range and directional moments. Shamir (2017, Theorem 1) allows an arbitrary proximal norm, while Gorbunov et al. (2022, Theorems 2.2 and 2.9, preprint version) give accelerated and non-accelerated smooth bounds with an ℓp proximal setup. Section N.4 states these guarantees, including their Bregman radii and finite-difference parameters. The objective regularity constants are M2 and L2, respectively. Two-point observations cost two scalar evaluations; running to expected error ε/ 3 yields the same fixed-budget success probability 2/3.

# 7 Conclusion and open questions 

We characterize exact-value complexity on the quadratic class throughout the stated accuracy window, on the full smooth class at moderate accuracy, and uniformly near μ = L. Our non-Euclidean lower bounds match the known smooth and nonsmooth rates in Corollaries 6.1 and 6.2. The common image profile separates regularity from localization, and actual regularizer sublevels retain the low-curvature directions that determine the spectral gains. The polynomial and multiscale constructions give complementary lower bounds whose max-imum applies to the full class. Determining the sharp joint dependence on accuracy and a growing condition number remains open. Further questions concern the complete image profile 10 for arbitrary norms, sharp complexity for known regularizers, and the coupling and dimension dependence of saddle-point complexity outside the matched plateaus. A joint characterization of scalar-value and partial-gradient budgets would extend the mixed-oracle results. Section R formulates these questions for the corresponding objective classes and gap criteria.

# Guide to the appendices 

Section A gives the detailed proof overview. The proof of the main minimization theorem occupies Section B: the inverse comparison is followed by the adaptive posterior calculation, its operator-norm budget, the radial moments, and the final calibration. Section C gives the upper bounds and the extension to prescribed strong convexity. Section D establishes logarithmic accuracy dependence for the full smooth class. Section E strengthens that observation to a dimension-times-depth bound and proves the matching near-endpoint theorem. Section N gives the upper–lower comparisons establishing the matching non-Euclidean rates in Section 6. Section F treats blockwise and mixed oracles, and Section G isolates the contribution of the coupling through Theorems G.1 and G.7. Section H compares saddle-point bounds according to their gap criteria and parameter ranges. Sections I to M contain the geometric statements and their proofs. The remaining appendices collect the literature comparison, the open questions, the numerical checks and protocols in Section S. Sections O and P include the all-p compatibility and fixed-reference extensions. The final appendix, Section T, compares our results with Wu et al. (2026). 15 Notation. In the geometry sections, μ denotes a strong-convexity modulus, λ the coefficient of the known penalty, and νi the eigenvalues of its Hessian. In the two-norm formulas, regularity is measured in ℓβ and localization in ℓα. Rm(B, K ) is the optimized image radius and rm(τ )

its specialization to the sublevel sets of a known penalty. In the posterior proof, M = EW and 

Q = EW −1 denote conditional matrix moments. In the geometry sections, Q is a linear map between normed spaces. Saddle-point gaps are defined with their compact or global optimization domains. We use x⋆ for optimal points, Q∗ for adjoints, and F ∗ for convex conjugates. 

# A Detailed information argument 

The proof tracks the conditional distribution of an unknown matrix. For symmetric matrices, 

A ⪯ B means that B − A is positive semidefinite, and ⟨A, B ⟩ = tr( AB ) is the Frobenius inner product. The hard quadratic family is parameterized by a Wishart matrix W , and each exact value encodes one rank-one linear measurement ⟨xx ⊤, W ⟩. After any history, the conditional density is the Wishart density restricted to the affine fiber determined by the observations. An inverse-moment comparison and a trace identity then bound the posterior operator norm using first moments alone, and an algorithm that optimizes successfully must correlate its output with 

W −1u strongly enough to violate that bound unless it has spent enough queries. The analytic statement holds uniformly over every affine fiber, which is why the query policy need not satisfy a linear-span restriction or keep its queries in a bounded set.

A.2 The adaptive posterior and its operator-norm bound 

Let X ∈ Rn×d have independent standard Gaussian entries, where n = d + m for an integer 

m > 3, put g = m − 1, and let W = X⊤X. For a history Hq of at most q scalar quadratic-form observations, including the independent seed, set Z = gW −1, M = E[W | H q], J = E[Z | H q],and let r be the dimension of span {xix⊤

Each nonredundant query is a linear functional fixed by the preceding history, and its coarea factor is constant along the next fiber, so the 16 conditional law is again the Wishart density restricted to the realized affine fiber. Theorem A.1 and a centered divergence identity therefore give 

J ⪯ nM −1, tr( M J ) = nd − 2r, r ≤ q. (21) Put A = M/n and Υ = 12 tr( A + J) − tr( AJ ), a scalar defect that vanishes only when J = I. If 

bi > 0 are the eigenvalues of J and ai the corresponding diagonal entries of A, then 0 < a i ≤ 1/b i,and the terms Υi = ( ai + bi)/2 − aibi are nonnegative, with Υi ≥ (bi − 1) 2/(2 bi) once bi ≥ 1/2.Applied to the largest eigenvalue, this gives ∥J∥op ≤ ϕ(Υ) , where ∥ · ∥ op is the ℓ2 → ℓ2 operator norm and ϕ(v) = 1 + v + √v2 + 2 v. The tower identities EA = EJ = I together with (21) give 

EΥ = 2 Er/n , and since ϕ is increasing and concave, Jensen’s inequality yields

A.3 Prior validity, amplification, and the explicit constant

To pass to Ω, consider the smooth strictly convex function 

ϕ(t) = ∥t∥22 + tr( W (t)−1).

It diverges at infinity and on approach to any finite boundary point of Ω, it has a unique minimizer, and every level above the minimum is regular. The sublevel sets Db = {ϕ < b }

are therefore smooth bounded convex domains with closure in Ω, and they exhaust Ω. By dominated convergence, the assumed moments give MDb → M and QDb → Q, normalizing constants included, since tr W and tr W −1 dominate all relevant matrix entries. Since M ≻ 0,inversion is continuous at M , and the positive-semidefinite order is closed. This proves (26). A zero-dimensional slice is a point mass and satisfies the assertion directly. Uniform ellipticity is needed only on each individual compact domain. For sharpness, take the full cone, C = I/ 2, and n = 2 ζ + d + 1 . The determinant-power (Wishart) density then has M = nI and Q = I/ (2 ζ), also for the corresponding real degrees-of-freedom parameter, so equality holds in (26). This completes the proof of Theorem B.1. 

B.2 The posterior on an adaptive observation fiber 

Let X ∈ Rn×d have independent standard Gaussian entries, where n = d + m and m > 3 are integers, and put 

W = X⊤X, g = m − 1, Z = gW −1, s(W ) = I − Z

2 .

The density of W on Sym ++  

> d

is proportional to det( W )g/ 2 exp( − tr W/ 2) , and s is its negative score with respect to the Frobenius metric. Let H include the independent algorithmic seed and the complete history of at most q adaptive observations x⊤ 

> i

W x i, and define 

M = E[W | H ], J = E[Z | H ].

The prior moments are EW = nI and EW −1 = I/g , so that EM = nI and EJ = I. The inverse mean also follows from the Schur-complement law derived below: for any fixed unit vector u,

u⊤W −1u is distributed as 1/χ 2

> m+1

.

Lemma B.3 (Conditional law on the realized fiber) . Let S = span {xix⊤ 

> i

: 1 ≤ i ≤ q} for the realized history, and let r = dim S. Almost surely, the conditional law of W is its original density normalized on the realized affine slice W0 + S⊥ intersected with Sym ++  

> d

.Proof. First condition on the independent seed; by independence, the prior density is unchanged. Given a partial history, the next query x is fixed, and its answer is the linear functional 

ℓx(W ) = ⟨xx ⊤, W ⟩ on the current affine slice. If the projection of xx ⊤ onto the tangent space of the slice is nonzero, the linear coarea formula disintegrates affine Lebesgue measure into Lebesgue measures on the codimension-one level sets. The coarea factor is the reciprocal norm of this projection; it is constant on each resulting fiber and cancels when the conditional density is normalized. If the projection is zero, the answer is already determined by the current history, and neither the conditional law nor the fiber changes. Induction therefore gives the stated conditional density. Explicitly, on each positive-dimensional resulting fiber F the kernel is 

P(W ∈ B | H ) = 

Z

> B∩F∩ Sym ++
> d

det( U )g/ 2e− tr U/ 2 dU 

Z

> F∩ Sym ++
> d

det( U )g/ 2e− tr U/ 2 dU 

21 at almost every realized history, and a singleton fiber carries a point mass. Previous query choices are determined by previous answers, so they introduce no additional weight varying within the fiber. Fixed-order Gram–Schmidt with zero pivots skipped gives measurable tangent bases and separates the measurable rank strata, and applying the Moore–Penrose inverse to the realized finite linear system gives measurable affine base points. Together, these constructions yield measurable versions of the successive kernels for Borel policies and random ranks. The argument applies the coarea formula to each fixed linear measurement after conditioning on its past; it requires neither differentiability nor continuity of the policy or of the full transcript map. 

Remark B.4 (General linear measurements) . The conditional-law proof and all posterior identities below hold unchanged for observations ⟨Bi, W ⟩ with arbitrary symmetric Bi chosen measurably from the preceding history. Replace the measured span by span {Bi}; each informative scalar observation still reduces the affine dimension by exactly one. Neither positivity nor the rank-one structure of xix⊤ 

> i

is used in these arguments. The rank-one restriction enters the optimization reduction only through the quadratic objective representation. 

Lemma B.5 (Inverse and trace identities on the conditional fiber) . Almost surely, 

J ⪯ nM −1, tr( M J ) = nd − 2r, r ≤ q. (31) 

Proof. By the tower property, the conditional first and inverse-first moments are finite almost surely. Applying Theorem B.1 with ζ = g/ 2 to the conditional law of Lemma B.3 and multiplying by g gives J ⪯ (g + d + 1) M −1 = nM −1.The conditional mean M lies in the same affine slice. In its v = d(d + 1) /2 − r orthonormal tangent coordinates, the centered vector field W − M has divergence v, so integration by parts gives 

v = E[⟨s(W ), W − M ⟩ | H ] = tr( M J ) − gd 

2 ,

where we used E[W − M | H ] = 0 and W −1W = I. Since gd + 2 v = nd − 2r, the trace identity follows. It remains to justify the boundary terms and the integrability in this calculation. Set 

ζ = g/ 2 > 1 and extend p(W ) = det( W )ζ e− tr W/ 2 by zero outside the positive-definite cone. Inside the cone, 

∇p = e− tr W/ 2



Both terms tend to zero at every finite boundary point, so the zero extension is C1. For positive-semidefinite W ,

∥W ∥F ≤ tr W, det W ≤ ∥ W ∥dF , ∥ adj W ∥op ≤ ∥ W ∥d−1 

> F

Hence p, (1 + ∥W ∥F )p, (1 + ∥W ∥F )∇p, and W −1p = adj W det( W )ζ−1e− tr W/ 2 are bounded on every fixed affine slice by integrable functions of the form polynomial times decaying exponential. Choose the affine base point orthogonal to the tangent space; then ∥W ∥F dominates the tangent-coordinate norm. Compact cutoffs therefore remove the boundary contribution at infinity, while the zero extension removes the flux through the finite boundary. This also gives finite forward and inverse means on every slice meeting the positive-definite cone. For a zero-dimensional slice, 

M = W and J = gW −1, and the identity follows directly. Finally, each scalar query reveals at most one new dimension, so r ≤ q.

B.6 Three validated runs give an unconditional correlation 

Run three independent copies of the original T -query algorithm on the same objective, query each returned point once, and select the output with the smallest observed value, breaking ties by index. The total number of calls is at most 

q = 3( T + 1) . (41) This is an admissible adaptive protocol with a tuple of independent seeds. Each run uses its own transcript and seed, so, conditional on a fixed objective, its failure is independent of the other runs. The three validation calls are part of the combined history.

The expectation is taken over the unconditioned Wishart prior and all seeds. Proof. B.7 Dimension baseline and the constant 4096 

Lemma B.12 (Adaptive dimension baseline) . Under the hypotheses of Theorem 2.1, T ≥ 5d/ 16 .This bound holds even for the positive-definite quadratics fθ(x) = L∥x − Rθ ∥22/2 with ∥θ∥2 = 1 .Proof. An informative query reveals one coordinate in a direction fixed by the past, whereas a redundant query reveals nothing. Remark B.13 (Moment requirements and proof dependencies) . The operator budget uses only conditional first moments. Together with the spectral estimates, the twelfth radial moment gives a validity event of probability at least 49 /50 ; conditioning on that event yields a fixed prior supported on the required class. The probabilistic reduction combines elliptic regularity, conditional disintegration, and Wishart integration. Optimizing the explicit constant 4096 

remains an open question. 

# C Upper bounds, moderate-accuracy optimality, and strong con-vexity 

C.1 The two quadratic upper bounds 

Write q(x) = 12 x⊤Hx + b⊤x + c with H symmetric. The value at zero determines c, and the values at ±ei give 

bi = q(ei) − q(−ei)2 , Hii = q(ei) + q(−ei) − 2q(0) .

For i < j ,

Hij = q(ei + ej ) − q(ei) − q(ej ) + q(0) .

This procedure uses exactly 1 + 2 d +  d

> 2

 = ( d + 1)( d + 2) /2 queries. Convexity implies H ⪰ 0,and the existence of a global minimizer implies b ∈ range( H). Hence −H†b is the minimum-norm global minimizer, and its norm is at most R. Pseudoinversion is a Borel map, since it is continuous on each of the finitely many fixed-rank strata. On inconsistent transcripts the policy may return zero. This defines a total Borel rule. For any fixed h̸ = 0 , expanding the quadratic also gives 

q(x + he i) − q(x − he i)2h = ( Hx + b)i.

28 Therefore 2d values recover the gradient exactly at any iterate. The accelerated method with known L and starting point zero satisfies q(yN )−min q ≤ 2LR 2/N 2 (Bubeck, 2015, Theorem 3.19), and N = ⌈p2LR 2/ε ⌉ gives the claimed budget of values. Both are fixed-budget deterministic algorithms and hence admissible in the randomized model. Selecting the cheaper procedure on the basis of d, L, R, ε proves the upper bound in Corollary 2.2; Theorem 2.1 supplies the lower bound. 

C.2 Upper bounds for the general smooth class 

The two procedures above rely on the fact that finitely many values identify a quadratic or recover its gradient exactly. For general f ∈ C d,L,R , neither property is guaranteed. The model imposes no precision limit, however, so central differences with an arbitrarily small step are available: for h > 0, the vector gh(x)i = [ f (x + he i) − f (x − he i)] /(2 h) requires 2d values and, since |∂if (x + te i) − ∂if (x)| ≤ L|t|, satisfies 

∥gh(x) − ∇ f (x)∥2 ≤ Lh √d/ 2 =: ∆ .

The acceleration bound below proves the upper bound in Corollary 2.3; together with Theorem 2.1, it is tight throughout LR 2/d 2 ≤ ε ≤ LR 2/32 . The cutting-plane bound gives a smaller upper bound for the general class at sufficiently high accuracy, where a matching dependence on dimension and accuracy remains open. 

Finite-difference acceleration, O(dpLR 2/ε ) values. On Q = Bd

f (yN ) − min f ≤ 2LR 2

> 2

Since 2R∆ ≤ ε/ 2, the best queried point is an ε-minimizer after N = O(d log(1 /α )) = 

O(d log( LR 2/ε )) steps of 2d + 1 values each. The same guarantee holds if the method stops early. Computing centroids is allowed, since the model does not restrict computation.

# D A nonquadratic obstruction at high accuracy 

The quadratic reconstruction bound of Corollary 2.2 relies on a finite parameterization of the objective. The following construction makes the distinction from the full smooth class explicit. Its logarithmic term combines with Corollary 2.4 by taking the maximum of the two lower bounds. Classical information-based complexity already studies logarithmic dependence on the accuracy (Nemirovski and Yudin, 1983); our purpose here is an explicit construction in the unrestricted exact-value model. 

Theorem D.1 (Fixed-dimensional high-accuracy bound) . Let d ≥ 1, 0 < μ < L , R > 0, and 

ρ = ( L − μ)/(128( L + μ)) . For every integer m ≥ 1 and every 0 < ε ≤ 2μR 2ρ2m, an algorithm with the success convention of Theorem 2.1, on the globally μ-strongly convex subclass of Cd,L,R ,requires T ≥ m/ 6 queries. Thus, for fixed κ = L/μ > 1, its complexity is Ωκ(log( μR 2/ε )) as

By strong convexity, every successful output lies within p2ε/μ ≤ 2Rρ m = αr m−1/2 of its minimizer. The sets of successful outputs for different sign sequences are therefore disjoint, including at the endpoint of the stated accuracy window. Now draw the signs independently and uniformly before drawing the algorithm’s seed, and consider an enhanced oracle that reveals a prefix of the sign sequence and can simulate every exact value. If j < m signs are known and a query x lies outside Ij , all remaining modifications vanish at x. If x ∈ Ij , reveal σj+1 and continue until x leaves the next child interval or all signs are known. The revealed modifications can then be evaluated, and the unrevealed ones vanish at 

x, so this rule determines fσ(x) exactly. Using closed intervals only makes the enhanced oracle more informative at endpoints. Conditional on the seed and the enhanced history, the unrevealed signs remain independent and uniform: at every disclosure, both the decision to continue and the numerical answer depend only on the prefix already disclosed, so no condition is imposed on the remaining suffix. A query can belong to at most one of the two disjoint children, so its conditional probability of reaching each further level is at most 1/2, and its expected number of new signs is at most P 

> k≥0

2T /m . If JT < m , at least two equally likely sign sequences remain, and their sets of successful outputs are disjoint, so any history-measurable output succeeds with conditional probability at most 1/2. Therefore, 

P{success } ≤ 12 + 12 P{JT = m} ≤ 12 + Tm .

The required instancewise success probability implies average success probability at least 2/3

under this prior, proving T ≥ m/ 6. All query comparisons and polynomial evaluations are Borel, and arbitrary queries outside the support disclose nothing further. 31 For d > 1, use fσ(x1) + ( μ/ 2) Pdi=2 x2

# E Multiscale lower bounds and matching fine-accuracy complexity 

We prove Theorem 2.5 and the dimension-times-logarithm lower bound by a nested smooth construction. The class, exact-value oracle, success probability, and deterministic worst-case budget are those of Section 2. A nonnegative modulus μ < L is prescribed; at μ = 0 only convexity is required. The hidden directions are sampled before the independent algorithmic seed. Every query and the output may be any finite vector. The parameters a, α, ρ specify the curvature, perturbation amplitude, and scale contraction in this construction.

E.2 A first-hit argument that counts cascades 

The following spherical recovery estimate bounds the probability of a first entry into a hidden child. 

Lemma E.3 (Adaptive spherical recovery) . Let ξ be uniform on Sd−1. After at most t < d 

adaptive scalar linear measurements, any Borel estimator, with independent randomization allowed, has probability at most 12 + t

2d(1 − s)

of recovering ξ with squared error at most s ∈ (0 , 1) .

34 Proof. Orthogonalize the informative queries and supplement dependent queries by a measurable orthogonal completion. Conditional on the transcript, the unobserved component is uniform on the residual sphere. If Ui is its squared radius after i informative measurements, rotational invariance gives E[Ui+1 | H i] = Ui(d − i − 1) /(d − i). Hence EUt = ( d − t)/d . When Ut > s ,a radius-√s ball contains at most one point from each antipodal pair of the residual sphere, and conditional success is at most 1/2. Otherwise the observed squared norm is at least 1 − s.Markov’s inequality bounds the probability of the latter event by t/ [d(1 − s)] . Averaging proves the claim. Orthogonal completion can test the standard coordinate vectors in their fixed order, so all maps are Borel. The antipodal argument includes the two-point sphere. 

Lemma E.4 (First entry into a hidden child) . In Theorem E.1, let the directions be independent and uniform on Sd−1, and put s = ( ρ/α )2 ≤ 1/16 . There is an oracle more informative than exact values for which the number Nj of queries processed at level j, including its first child hit, obeys 

P{Nj ≤ k | causal history before level j} ≤ 12 + k − 12d(1 − s) , 1 ≤ k ≤ d. 

Proof. At the first unrevealed level j, a query x receives ⟨ξj , x − cj−1⟩. If x is outside the child 

B(cj , r j ), processing stops. If it is inside, the oracle reveals the whole direction and processes the same point at the next level. The query may therefore cascade through several levels. Child membership is already determined by the supplied linear measurement, since 

∥x − cj ∥22 = ∥x − cj−1∥22 + α2r2 

> j−1

+ 2 αr j−1⟨ξj , x − cj−1⟩.

The response determines the original function value: revealed perturbations are known, the current perturbation is computed from its projection, and all deeper perturbations vanish where processing stops. The smooth cutoff also vanishes on a support boundary. Thus no extra nonlinear measurement is hidden in the child indicator. At the instant a new level is first processed, its direction is independent of the causal past. The causal past ends immediately before the level’s first internal oracle operation, including when that operation occurs within a cascade. Simulate the first k − 1 measurements at that level. If an earlier child entry occurs, its measurement identifies the entry; return −(x − cj−1)/(αr j−1) and stop before the full-direction disclosure. If no entry occurs, return the same candidate formed from the kth query point without requesting its answer. On {Nj ≤ k} this recovers ξj with squared error less than s. Lemma E.3 applies with t = k − 1. The recovery estimate applies directly to this stopped experiment under the direction’s original conditional law.

# F Saddle-point models, blockwise and mixed information bounds 

F.1 Models and blockwise information bounds 

Let dx, d y ≥ 1, Li, R i > 0, and 0 ≤ μi ≤ Li for i ∈ { x, y }. The objective F : Rdx × Rdy → R is globally differentiable, μx-strongly convex in x and μy-strongly concave in y; a zero modulus means ordinary convexity in x, respectively ordinary concavity in y. The partial gradients satisfy 

∥∇ xF (x, y ) − ∇ xF (x′, y )∥2 ≤ Lx∥x − x′∥2,

∥∇ yF (x, y ) − ∇ yF (x, y ′)∥2 ≤ Ly∥y − y′∥2, (47) 

∥∇ xF (x, y ) − ∇ xF (x, y ′)∥2 ≤ Lxy ∥y − y′∥2,

∥∇ yF (x, y ) − ∇ yF (x′, y )∥2 ≤ Lxy ∥x − x′∥2.

The smoothness and coupling constants are upper bounds, whereas the strong-convexity and strong-concavity moduli are lower bounds; in particular, the class permits zero coupling even when Lxy > 0. We require a global saddle point (x⋆, y ⋆) with ∥x⋆∥2 ≤ Rx and ∥y⋆∥2 ≤ Ry.The localized model takes X = RxBdx

The two models impose different output guarantees on the same class of objectives. We give the corresponding gap comparisons separately. Queries are unrestricted in both models. As in Section 2, policies are total Borel maps, seeds are independent, and an ε-accurate output must be returned with probability at least 2/3 on every instance. Theorem F.2 (A scalar-value lower bound in the mixed model) . Suppose that arbitrary exact partial-gradient queries ∇xF (x, y ) are available in addition to exact scalar values. Let N0 count every returned scalar value, including the values bundled with a partial-gradient call. Then 

N0 ≥ B y(ε) whenever the y index is admissible, regardless of the number of pure partial-gradient calls. With x and y interchanged, N0 ≥ B x(ε) holds in the other mixed model. Proof. Take Fg(x, y ) = μx∥x∥22/2 − g(y). Every partial-gradient response ∇xFg(x, y ) = μxx

is independent of g and is computed without any source query, whereas each scalar response requires one value of g. The same gap identity as before proves the claim. The simulation is adaptive in both types of response and reuses the algorithm’s own seed, so the induced source policy is again a total Borel map with the same budget. 

Remark F.3 (Outer accuracy and partial-gradient information) . The mixed-oracle theorem concerns a returned pair with a small gap. On its hard family, Φg(x) = sup y Fg(x, y ) = 

μx∥x∥22/2 − min g, so x = 0 minimizes Φg without any observation. The coupled construction below makes the outer solution depend on the unknown inner solution. In a bilinear model, an observed partial gradient contains A⊤y or Ax and thus reveals coupling information. In the mixed-oracle construction, the coupling is known and the unknown inner objective contains the hidden information. 

F.2 A coupled construction for mixed information and outer accuracy 

The hard family of the preceding mixed-oracle reduction is separable. The next construction uses a nonzero known coupling, so that the unknown inner solution determines the outer solution while the partial-gradient response remains independent of the unknown objective.

Corollary F.6 (Compact gaps when the minimizing responses are feasible) . The pair-gap conclusions of Theorems F.4 and F.5 also hold for feasible outputs and the gap on the balls X, Y

F.3 Block geometry and known regularizers 

Let Ki be nonempty compact convex sets, let ∥ · ∥ i be independently chosen regularity norms with unit balls Bi, and let ψi : Ki → R be known continuous convex functions that are nonnegative with minimum value zero. In the constrained composite model, let gx, g y be globally convex and consider 

F (x, y ) = gx(x) + λxψx(x) − gy(y) − λyψy(y), λx, λ y ≥ 0.

The regularity bounds apply only to the unknown terms; a known regularizer need not be smooth. The oracle returns gx(x) − gy(y) at unrestricted queries; equivalently, it may return F wherever both known penalty values are defined, and unrestricted values of F require specified finite extensions of the penalties. Outputs lie in Kx × Ky, and the gap is taken over these sets; it equals the sum of the two composite suboptimalities. For i ∈ { x, y }, 1 ≤ m ≤ di and τi ≥ 0, write

Retain the unknown term in one block and set the unknown term of the other block to zero. The composite term of the other block is then known and has minimum value zero. The gap dominates the active composite suboptimality. Subtracting the known term simulates one scalar query to the target by one source value, and Theorems 3.2 and 3.3 apply. In the mixed model, the partial derivatives in the other block are independent of the unknown source. A strongly convex ψi supplies the corresponding strong convexity or concavity of the composite objective. Smoothness of the full objective additionally requires a smoothness bound on the known penalty. An injective pullback preserves strong convexity after norm scaling; a lower-dimensional pullback requires a regularizer that also controls its kernel to obtain ambient strong convexity. In Corollary F.7, the stated regularity constants concern the unknown terms. 

# G Coupled saddle-point reductions with unequal block dimensions 

Throughout this section, r = min {dx, d y}, and U : Rr → Rdx and V : Rr → Rdy are fixed linear isometries. All algorithms follow the conventions of Theorem 2.1 on Borel measurability, independent randomization, fixed query budgets, and unrestricted queries. Each exact scalar query is simulated by a fixed number of evaluations of a source quadratic. This simulation cost determines the constants in the resulting lower bounds.

Proposition G.2 (Two upper bounds for compact bilinear games) . For the entire bilinear-affine class of Theorem G.1, there is a deterministic exact-value algorithm with query complexity 

O



min 



dy(dx + 1) , (dx + dy)



1 + CR xRy

ε

 

.

In particular, if dx = dy = d and 0 < ε ≤ CR xRy/(32 d2), the randomized minimax query complexity is Θ( d2).Proof. The values at (0 , e j ) recover bj , and the values at (ei, e j ) then recover Aji ; this uses exactly dy(dx + 1) scalar queries. Once the coefficients are known, the compact gap is the explicit continuous function 

Ry∥Ax − b∥2 + Rx∥A⊤y∥2 + b⊤y. 

A saddle point exists by compact convex minimax duality, and the displayed gap vanishes there. Fix an enumeration of the countable dense set of rational points of int X × int Y and return the first pair whose displayed gap is smaller than ε. Since the gap is continuous and int X × int Y is dense in X × Y , some enumerated pair has gap below ε, so the search terminates. This is a Borel rule, because each stopping condition is a strict inequality in the observed coefficients; inconsistent transcripts are assigned the output zero, which makes the rule total. Internal computation is unrestricted in the present oracle model. Alternatively, rescale x = Rxu, y = Ryv to the unit balls. The monotone saddle-point operator then has Lipschitz constant at most CR xRy, independently of the affine term, and Euclidean Mirror-Prox (Nemirovski, 2004) attains gap O(CR xRy/N ) after N iterations on this bounded domain. Since the payoff is affine in each coordinate, forward differences recover both partial gradients exactly from at most dx + dy + 1 values per operator evaluation. Each Mirror-Prox iteration uses two operator evaluations, so N = O(CR xRy/ε ) iterations cost 

O(( dx + dy)(1 + CR xRy/ε )) values in total. Choosing the cheaper of the two procedures on the basis of the known parameters proves the displayed bound. For equal dimensions the first branch is O(d2), and Theorem G.1 gives the matching order in the stated window. 

Proposition G.3 (Recovery under the global-saddle promise) . On the subclass in Theorem G.1 with a global saddle (x⋆, 0) and ∥x⋆∥2 ≤ Rx, a randomized algorithm recovers a zero-gap pair from dx(dx + 1) values, almost surely on every fixed instance. This query count is independent of 

dy.Proof. The promise implies b = Ax ⋆. Sample a matrix Q ∈ Rdx×dy with independent standard Gaussian entries and write q⊤ 

> j

for its rows. The values at (0 , q j ) and (ei, q j ) recover Qb and QA 

in dx(dx + 1) queries. For each fixed A, the restriction of Q to range A is injective almost surely: in any fixed orthonormal basis of this space it is a Gaussian matrix with at most dx columns, which has full column rank almost surely. Consequently, 

{x : QAx = Qb } = {x : Ax = b}

almost surely. The minimum-norm solution, computed by the Moore–Penrose inverse, then has norm at most Rx and solves Ax = b; paired with y = 0 , it has zero gap. The pseudoinverse is Borel, and on histories whose computed solution is infeasible or violates the compressed equations we return zero. This defines a total Borel policy with the same budget and the stated instancewise probability-one guarantee. 43 The promise is essential for this compressed recovery rule. The upper bound of Proposition G.2 applies to the full compact bilinear-affine class, including functions without a global saddle, and the lower bound of Theorem G.1 holds on both classes. For example, when dx = 1 the promised subclass is solvable with two values regardless of dy, so its complexity admits no uniform lower bound proportional to the dual dimension. The theorem isolates the dependence on the interaction; Section H compares the lower bound with the available upper bounds for compact bilinear games.

Recovery with partial-gradient information. For the shared-curvature family, the ad-ditional response ∇xF reveals h at (x, y ) = (0 , 0) and every column of H at the queries

H and all coordinates of h simultaneously. Thus partial-gradient access identifies the unknown coefficients of these hard families. The mixed-oracle constructions in Section F instead keep the coupling known and place the unknown information in the inner objective.

Proposition G.10 (Reconstruction with known diagonal curvatures) . An arbitrary quadratic with known diagonal Hessians μxI, −μyI, an unknown bilinear coupling, affine terms, and a constant can be reconstructed from 1 + dx + dy + dxdy exact scalar values. Its global saddle point can then be determined exactly. Consequently, when dx = dy = d, C ≥ 4d2√μxμy, and 

0 < ε ≤ μxρ2/8 with ρ as in Theorem G.7, the global-gap minimax complexity of that class, with the specified saddle-point localization, is Θ( d2).Proof. The value at (0 , 0) determines the constant. The values at (ei, 0) and (0 , e j ) determine the two affine terms after the known diagonal quadratics are subtracted. Finally, 

Aji = F (ei, e j ) − F (ei, 0) − F (0 , e j ) + F (0 , 0) 

determines the coupling. The stationarity equations have a unique solution, since eliminating 

y gives the positive-definite matrix μxI + A⊤A/μ y; solving them is a continuous operation in the coefficients and yields the unique global saddle point. The query count is as claimed. Under the displayed coupling condition, 12

q

C/ √μxμy ≥ d, so Theorem G.7 gives a lower bound of the same order.

# H Saddle-point complexity: comparisons and remaining gaps 

Our saddle-point results distinguish the dimensions dx and dy, the information returned by each query, and the set over which each best response is taken. We compare upper and lower bounds with these three specifications fixed. In this section, N0 counts scalar values and Nx counts partial gradients ∇xF ; a value returned together with a gradient contributes to both counts. For 

μx, μ y > 0, write 

κx = Lx

μx

, κy = Ly

μy

, χ = Lxy 

√μxμy

.

Here Lx, L y are the within-block constants denoted by Lxx , L yy in several of the cited papers. The notation eO suppresses logarithmic factors in the accuracy, the confidence, and the geometric and conditioning parameters of the cited result. Polynomial dependence on dimensions and condition numbers is displayed explicitly. 

Scalar-value and mixed extragradient bounds. In the exact deterministic specialization of Sadiev et al. (2021, Theorem 2 and Corollary 2), coordinate differences give the first row of Table 3; here D is the Euclidean diameter parameter and L bounds the full saddle-point operator. Their strongly convex–strongly concave bound is O(( dx + dy)( L/μ ) log(1 /ε )) scalar calls, with the initial-distance factors of the cited result inside the logarithm; its criterion is the expected squared distance to a saddle point. Their mixed result (Sadiev et al., 2021, Theorem 3 and Corollary 3) uses values in x and gradients in y, with O(dxLD 2/ε ) scalar calls and O(LD 2/ε )

partial-gradient calls for the expected gap. These estimates allow unequal block dimensions. Finite differences require function values in a neighborhood of the feasible sets, which is consistent with our unrestricted query model, but the compact-gap and global-gap output criteria remain distinct. A bound on the expectation of a nonnegative error yields a fixed-confidence guarantee by Markov’s inequality at a constant-factor loss in the target accuracy; the controlled quantity is unchanged. 

Accelerated mixed methods and their accuracy criteria. Gladin et al. (2021, Theorem 5) obtain the small-outer-block rates in Table 3 for Φ( x) = max y F (x, y ). Their error criterion is 

Φ( bx) − min Φ . Theorem 7 supplies the accelerated rates together with an explicit target failure probability. Its Catalyst construction gives the scalar-value budget eO(dy

pκxκy + 2 χ2) in Table 3. Its analysis controls the nested objectives and converts their errors into the dual objective error by means of Lemma 4. A pair-gap comparison would in addition require control of the primal objective error: writing X for the outer feasible set of the cited problem, 

Gap X, Rdy (bx, by) = Φ( bx) − Φ( x⋆) + Ψ( y⋆) − Ψ( by), Ψ( y) = min  

> x∈X

Known isotropic diagonal Hessians; global gap in a stated accuracy window; balanced-block recovery matches when χ ≥ 4d2.Theorems F.4 and F.5 Scalar-value lower bound with arbitrary ∇xF calls and known coupling Pair gap, or outer error for the shared-rank construction; the rectangular variant retains the full inner dimension under its curvature condition. 

The separable family in Theorem F.2 gives a pair-gap lower bound with a known outer minimizer (Remark F.3). For the outer objective error, Theorem F.4 uses a coupled construction, with the parameter dependence detailed below. 

Small blocks and composite information. Alkousa et al. (2023, Definition 1 and Theorem 2) study r(x) + F (x, y ) − h(y) and use the distance to the saddle point. For a low-dimensional x

block, their Vaidya-based construction uses eO(dx) evaluations of ∇xF and ∇r, and eO(dx

pLy/μ y)

evaluations of ∇yF and of the proximal map of h. When h is smooth instead, the budget of its own gradients is separated using its smoothness constant. The roles of the two blocks can be interchanged. These results motivate retaining both dimensions and separate oracle budgets, but proximal and vector responses provide more information than a scalar value. Our known-regularizer transfer (Corollary F.7) therefore addresses a different information model for the unknown smooth or Lipschitz term. 50 First-order lower bounds. For smooth strongly convex–strongly concave problems, Zhang et al. (2022) establish an iteration lower bound of order 

q

κx + χ2 + κy log(1 /ε )

in their first-order model, together with a proximal bilinear analogue. Their orthogonal-invariance argument extends beyond the initial linear-span restriction for deterministic algorithms. Their dimension requirement grows with the target iteration count; our scalar-value bounds keep 

(dx, d y) explicit. For bilinearly coupled objectives, Borodich et al. (2025, Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 4.8) characterize gradient evaluations and matrix–vector products separately in a first-order memory model. In the strongly convex–strongly concave specialization, the respective orders are √κx log(1 /ε ), √κy log(1 /ε ), and χ log(1 /ε ), and the error criterion is a weighted squared distance. Finite differences turn these gradient-based algorithms into scalar-value upper bounds. The corresponding scalar lower bounds require an information argument for each fixed pair of block dimensions. 

Coupling dependence proved here. Theorem G.1 gives a scalar-value lower bound even when both within-block Hessians vanish: 

Ω r min 

(

r, 

r Lxy RxRy

ε

)! 

, r = min {dx, d y}.

For the entire bilinear-affine class, Proposition G.2 supplies the upper bound 

O



min 



dy(dx + 1) , (dx + dy)



1 + Lxy RxRy

ε

 

.

The first branch identifies the payoff coefficients, and the second uses exact coordinate differences and Mirror-Prox. Consequently, the compact-gap minimax complexity is Θ( d2) when dx =

dy = d and 0 < ε ≤ Lxy RxRy/(32 d2). For shared-curvature quadratics, Theorem G.4 instead introduces the effective scale a + c2/b in the global gap, together with the curvature parameter 

θ = max {μx/a, μ y/b }. For arbitrary radii, Corollary G.5 uses the feasible truncated response and the scale Sx = a + 2 cβ − bβ 2 with β = min {c/b, R y/(2 Rx)}; this scale equals Ex when 

Ry ≥ 2cR x/b , and Corollary G.6 also recovers Ex within a local accuracy window. These results give interaction-dependent lower bounds for exact scalar values. Outside the matching regime, a gap remains between the square-root accuracy dependence of the compact bilinear lower bound and the inverse-linear dependence of the extragradient upper bound. The sharp dimension dependence for highly asymmetric blocks also remains open. The distinction between compact existence and a global-saddle promise also matters: on the latter subclass, Proposition G.3 gives almost-sure exact recovery from dx(dx + 1) values, independently of dy.The known-diagonal construction of Theorem G.7 keeps Lx/μ x = Ly/μ y = 1 and es-tablishes a lower bound of Ω( r min {r, √χ}) scalar calls for χ ≥ 4 and ε ≤ μxρ2/8, where 

ρ = min {Rx, 4pμy/μ xRy}. The theorem also states a wider global accuracy window, and Corollary G.9 gives the compact-gap conditions. The reconstruction in Proposition G.10 matches the lower bound at Θ( d2) for equal blocks, χ ≥ 4d2, and 0 < ε ≤ μxρ2/8. Outside this plateau, the dependence on conditioning and accuracy remains open. Proposition G.11 identifies the accuracy limitation of the primal-coordinate decoder used in this construction. 

Mixed information with known coupling. Unlike the separable mixed family, Theorem F.4 makes the outer solution depend on an unknown inner quadratic. Its partial-gradient responses are nevertheless known, so the pair-gap and outer-error lower bounds (50)–(51) allow arbitrary pure 

∇xF calls. The source condition number (Ly + c2/α )/(μy + c2/α ) decreases as the coupling grows. 51 Thus stronger coupling improves the conditioning of this inner problem. The complementary Theorem F.5 retains the full dimension dy for a fixed rectangular coupling when μy + c2/α ≤ Ly,for the pair-gap criterion. This criterion controls the inner components in the kernel of the coupling, which the outer solution alone leaves undetermined. Corollary F.6 supplies a common sufficient condition for compact pair gaps. Matching the nested upper bounds in Table 3 under each accuracy criterion, and determining the joint trade-off between scalar-value and partial-gradient budgets, remain open. 

Accuracy saturation and unresolved comparisons. Corollary 2.2 characterizes scalar 

minimization on quadratics, and the saddle-point comparisons above give matching bounds in two plateau regimes with equal block dimensions. The necessity of the logarithmic accuracy factors in the cited general-class upper bounds remains open in the unmatched regimes. Aquadratic in dx + dy variables can be interpolated from O(d2 

> x

+ dxdy + d2

> y

) values, after which its saddle-point problem can be solved without further oracle information; this explains why finite-dimensional quadratic lower bounds saturate. Obtaining the optimal coupling dependence, determining the role of the two dimensions beyond the shared subspace, and proving sharp mixed-budget trade-offs require further arguments. 

# I Independent regularity norms, localization sets, and known regularizers 

A non-Euclidean formulation has three ingredients that may be chosen independently . We first fix the norm or set attached to each regularity constant and radius. • The regularity norm ∥ · ∥ , with unit ball B and dual norm ∥ · ∥ ∗. It measures the regularity of the unknown function: ∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥∗ ≤ L∥x − y∥ for all x, y in the smooth case, and |g(x) − g(y)| ≤ M ∥x − y∥ in the nonsmooth case. • The localization set K ⊂ Rd, a nonempty compact convex set. In the smooth model, a global minimizer lies in K and queries are unrestricted; in the nonsmooth model, the minimum is taken over K and the returned point must lie in K.• The known regularizer ψ : K → R, continuous and convex with min K ψ = 0 , which enters the objective as λψ with λ ≥ 0. Its values and proximal operations are free. Only the unknown summand is accessed through exact values, and its regularity is bounded by 

L or M . Setting λ = 0 recovers the unregularized models. Throughout, the symbol μ is reserved for a strong-convexity modulus. The accuracy ε is measured in objective values . The regularity norm B and localization set K

can vary independently: the example below combines a feasible ℓ1 ball with a gradient that is Lipschitz with respect to ℓ2.

The common transfer framework. The definition of Rm(B, C ) and the three transfer theorems are stated in the main text, Equation (5) and Theorems 3.1 to 3.3. The smooth proofs are in Section J, and the nonsmooth regularized proof is in Section M. We specialize these results to independently chosen regularity and localization norms. 

Balls: an exact full-dimensional radius. Take B = Bdβ and K = Bdα(Rα) with 1 ≤ α, β ≤ ∞ 

chosen independently. The subscripts below identify the norm attached to each constant: 

∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥β∗ ≤ Lβ ∥x − y∥β , |g(x) − g(y)| ≤ Mβ ∥x − y∥β , ∥x⋆∥α ≤ Rα. (54) 52 For the comparison that follows, we use the minimal valid smoothness moduli of a fixed objective and take each radius to be the corresponding norm of one chosen minimizer. The complexity theorems allow any independently specified admissible upper bounds on these quantities. The constants can differ for the same objective: Lβ is the largest curvature over the unit ℓβ ball, so 

L1 ≤ L2 ≤ L∞ with each successive ratio at most d, whereas R1 ≥ R2 ≥ R∞ with R1 ≤ √d R 2

(Remark L.1). The accuracy ε remains independent of the norm.

The exponent vanishes exactly when β ≤ 2 ≤ α, that is, when the feasible unit ball contains the Euclidean unit ball and the regularity unit ball is contained in the Euclidean unit ball; otherwise, an increase of 1/α above 1/2 and a decrease of 1/β below 1/2 contribute equally to the exponent. The block factor ⌊d/m ⌋γ is nontrivial only for β < α , and it acts differently on the smooth and on the nonsmooth bound. Write Ψ = γ + θ ≤ 1. In the smooth case the product term is largest at m = d (Corollary L.5): a block map can widen the accuracy window but cannot improve the bound when the full-dimensional certificate is admissible. The nonsmooth profile changes behavior at Ψ = 1 /2, so for Ψ > 1/2 and (Mβ Rα/ε )2 < d 1+2 θ an interior block dimension can improve the bound relative to m = d (Corollary I.2). No single certificate is optimal in every parameter regime. Euclidean sections give radius ≍ Rα at a smaller dimension, which at α = β = ∞ improves (56) by ≍ √d (Section J). The next result records the bounds obtained from a single certificate, uniformly in (α, β ).

# J Euclidean images and sublevels of known regularizers 

This appendix proves the norm and known-regularizer transfers of Theorems 3.1 and 3.2, the spectral corollary in Corollary 3.4, and the geometric examples. All smooth information bounds below rest on Theorem 2.1 alone, with C⋆ = 4096 . The geometric statements compute or bound the image radius Rm(B, C ) of (5). Section 6 identifies the resulting matching complexity regimes. 56 J.1 Attainment, centers, and smooth oracle transfers 

Let B be the unit ball of a norm ∥ · ∥ on Rd, and let ∥ · ∥ ∗ be its dual norm. For Q : Rd → Rm,write ∥Q∥B→2 = sup x∈B ∥Qx ∥2. Its Euclidean adjoint satisfies 

∥Q∗u∥∗ ≤ ∥ Q∥B→2∥u∥2. (66) Indeed, the left-hand side equals sup x∈B ⟨u, Qx ⟩.

Lemma J.1 (Attainment and elementary invariances) . Let C ⊂ Rd be nonempty, compact, and convex, and let 1 ≤ m ≤ d. The maximum defining Rm(B, C ) is attained. For c ∈ Rd and t > 0,

Rm(B, c + tC ) = tRm(B, C ).

The radius is monotone in C under inclusion. If C = −C, optimizing over translated inscribed balls gives the same result as requiring their centers to be zero. A positive radius requires rank Q = m

for every certificate attaining the maximum, and Rm(B, C ) = 0 when m > dim aff C.Proof. First, the contractions form a compact subset of the finite-dimensional space of linear maps: the operator norm is continuous, and by norm equivalence the set is bounded in any matrix norm. Set M = max x∈C ∥x∥. Whenever z + rBm 

> 2

⊆ Q(C) and ∥Q∥B→2 ≤ 1, we have 

∥z∥2 ≤ M , because z ∈ Q(C), and 0 ≤ r ≤ M , because the diameter of Q(C) is at most 2M .Hence the feasible triples (Q, z, r ) lie in a fixed compact set. They also form a closed set: with 

hC (v) = max x∈C ⟨v, x ⟩, the containment is equivalent to 

hC (Q∗u) − ⟨ u, z ⟩ ≥ r∥u∥2 for every u ∈ Rm.

Each inequality defines a closed set because hC is continuous. The feasible set is nonempty, since it contains Q = 0 , z = 0 , r = 0 , so the maximum of r is attained. Next, translating or positively scaling C translates or scales every image accordingly, and inclusion gives monotonicity. If C = −C and z +rBm 

> 2

⊆ Q(C), then by reflection also −z +rBm 

> 2

⊆

Q(C), and taking midpoints, by convexity, gives rBm 

> 2

⊆ Q(C). Finally, a positive radius forces 

Q(C) to be full-dimensional in Rm, whereas its affine dimension is at most both rank Q and 

dim aff C.

Proof of Theorem 3.1. Fix an admissible m and set r = Rm(B, K ) > 0. By Lemma J.1, choose a fixed contraction Q and a fixed z ∈ Rm with z + rBm 

> 2

⊆ Q(K). For every convex, Euclidean 

L-smooth source objective q : Rm → R with a global minimizer u∗ in rBm 

> 2

, define 

fq(x) = q(Qx − z), x ∈ Rd.

This function is convex and differentiable, and by (66), 

∥∇ fq(x) − ∇ fq(y)∥∗ ≤ ∥∇ q(Qx − z) − ∇ q(Qy − z)∥2

≤ L∥Q(x − y)∥2 ≤ L∥x − y∥.

The image inclusion provides x∗ ∈ K with Qx ∗ − z = u∗, so x∗ is a global minimizer of fq and 

min fq = min q.Now suppose a target algorithm with budget T satisfies the required guarantee for every such 

fq. A source algorithm runs it with the same random seed. Whenever the target requests fq(x),the simulator requests the single source value q(Qx − z) and returns it unchanged; given the target output bx, it returns Qbx − z. The objective gaps agree exactly. The fixed affine query and output maps are continuous, so composing them with the target’s total Borel policies produces total Borel source policies with the same independent seed, budget, and success probability. All source queries and the decoded output are permitted, since the source model is unrestricted. The 57 lift establishes membership of fq in the target class; the query simulator and output decoder use the fixed affine map x 7 → Qx − z.Finally, apply Theorem 2.1 in dimension m, at radius r and accuracy ε, under the stated accuracy condition 0 < ε ≤ Lr 2/32 . This gives (7). Since every admissible dimension bounds the same target complexity, their maximum is also a lower bound. Positive-definite source quadratics suffice; their pullbacks are convex quadratics, which can have a kernel when m < d .

Proof of Theorem 3.2. Fix admissible m, τ , put r = Rm(B, K τ ) > 0 and δ = ε + λτ , and choose a fixed certificate z + rBm 

> 2

⊆ Q(Kτ ). For a Euclidean source objective q as in the preceding proof, define 

gq(x) = q(Qx − z), Fq(x) = gq(x) + λψ (x), x ∈ K. 

The same calculation as before shows that gq is globally convex and smooth in the specified norm. A source minimizer u∗ ∈ rBm 

> 2

has a lift x0 ∈ Kτ , so gq(x0) = min q and 0 ≤ ψ(x0) ≤ τ .For every feasible ε-optimal output, the nonnegativity of ψ on K gives 

q(Qbx − z) ≤ Fq(bx) ≤ min  

> x∈K

Fq(x) + ε

≤ Fq(x0) + ε ≤ min q + λτ + ε. 

Hence the same affine decoder is δ-accurate for the source. Each query to the globally defined unknown term gq is exactly one unrestricted source query. If the interface returns Fq on its query domain, the simulator adds the known value λψ (x);this requires a specified finite extension of the penalty wherever queries outside K are allowed. Conversely, subtracting that known value recovers gq(x) on this domain. Any free operations that depend only on the known penalty, set, and parameters are independent of q and can be built into the simulator; within the stated Borel algorithm model they do not affect the reduction. The measurability and fixed-budget argument of the preceding proof applies. Finally, δ > 0

and the assumed condition δ ≤ Lr 2/32 give exactly the source accuracy window, and applying Theorem 2.1 at accuracy δ proves (8). Taking the supremum over all admissible m, τ preserves this bound. 

Proposition J.2 (Affine normalization of a known penalty) . For a known affine function 

ℓ : Rd → R, define 

cℓ = min 

> x∈K

(ψ(x) − ℓ(x)) , ϕℓ(x) = ψ(x) − ℓ(x) − cℓ.

The smooth composite class with known penalty ψ is equivalent, up to known additive constants and one-for-one value simulation, to that with penalty ϕℓ. Consequently, (8) may also be optimized over these normalized penalties, subject to the admissibility condition for each sublevel. Proof. By compactness and continuity, cℓ is finite, and ϕℓ is convex, continuous, and nonnegative on K with minimum zero. Set eg = g + λℓ . Adding an affine function preserves convexity and the gradient Lipschitz constant in any norm, and 

g + λψ = eg + λϕ ℓ + λc ℓ.

The correspondence is a bijection on the unknown smooth class, with inverse g = eg − λℓ .Oracle values differ by the known affine term, while the two composite objectives have identical suboptimality gaps on K. It remains to apply Theorem 3.2 after this reparameterization. The same conclusion does not follow for a fixed global Lipschitz budget, since adding a nonconstant affine function can change that budget. 58 J.2 Exact ellipsoidal and quadratic-sublevel profiles 

Proposition J.3 (Ellipsoidal geometries) . Let P, S be positive-definite d × d matrices, let 

BP = {x : x⊤P x ≤ 1}, and let C = c + S1/2Bd

> 2

. If ν↓ 

> m

denotes the mth largest eigenvalue, then 

Rm(BP , C )2 = ν↓

> m

(P 1/2SP 1/2), 1 ≤ m ≤ d. (67) 

In particular, Euclidean smoothness and a feasible ellipsoid with semiaxes a1 ≥ · · · ≥ ad > 0 give 

Rm = am; matching geometries C = c + RB P give Rm = R for every m.Proof. By translation invariance we may drop c. The contraction condition reads ∥QP −1/2∥2→2 ≤

1, so write Q = AP 1/2 with ∥A∥2→2 ≤ 1, and put D = P 1/2SP 1/2. The image ellipsoid has shape matrix ADA ∗ and Euclidean inradius pνmin (ADA ∗), which is zero for a singular image. Choosing the rows of A to be orthonormal eigenvectors associated with the largest eigenvalues of D gives the lower bound in (67). For the reverse bound, take any such A and choose a unit vector u ∈ Rm such that A∗u is orthogonal to the top m − 1 eigenvectors of D; such a vector exists because these are at most m − 1 homogeneous linear constraints. J.3 An unbounded gain over the scalar curvature majorant 

We compare two lower-bound expressions for the same known-composite class. A bound based on the scalar majorant ψ(x) ≤ νd∥x∥22/2, where νd is the largest eigenvalue of H, certifies only the centered balls

Optimizing over all linear images of these certified balls, all dimensions, and all admissible levels therefore gives a precise scalar-majorant benchmark. The calculation below keeps the dimension baseline, the saturation cap, and the validity window in both expressions.

# K Geometric transfers and nonsmooth strong convexity 

K.1 Specializations to ℓp balls 

Fix 1 ≤ p ≤ 2, let q be its conjugate exponent, and put Γ = d2/p −1 and Bdp(R) = {x : ∥x∥p ≤ R}.We distinguish global smooth optimization, with unrestricted queries and a global minimizer in this ball, from nonsmooth optimization, with queries and output restricted to the ball. All complexities use the fixed-budget and success-probability conventions of Theorem 2.1. Both transfers are query-preserving: one source value answers one target value. The smooth transfer is a direct corollary of Theorem 2.1; the nonsmooth transfer carries an external Euclidean bound, stated explicitly as Proposition K.6, over to non-Euclidean geometry. In the notation of (54), this appendix treats the diagonal case α = β = p: the constants L, M and R below stand for Lp,

Mp and Rp, respectively, where Lp acts from ℓp to ℓq, Mp is the Lipschitz modulus with respect to ℓp, and Rp is an ℓp radius (Remark L.1). 

Smooth geometry and strong convexity. Let Gp(d, L, R ) be the class of globally convex functions that satisfy ∥∇ f (x) − ∇ f (y)∥q ≤ L∥x − y∥p and have a global minimizer in Bdp(R). Its 

μ-strongly convex subclass, with strong convexity relative to ℓp, is denoted by Gp,μ (d, L, R ). Write 

Sε for the unrestricted exact-value complexity. Without strong convexity, Sε(Gp) is bounded below by (57) at α = β = p, where d2θ = Γ . Adding a prescribed modulus gives the following. 

Corollary K.1 (Smooth norm transfer under strong convexity) . For L ≥ μΓ > 0 and 0 < ε ≤

LR 2/(32Γ) ,

Sε(Gp,μ ) ≥ max 

(

The condition L ≥ μΓ is exactly the threshold for the class to be nonempty. At equality, every objective has the form L∥x∥22/2 + b⊤x + c, and d + 1 values suffice for exact optimization (Theorem K.16).

Lemma K.3 (Exact online reduction) . Fix a map from source objectives to objectives in a target class. Suppose that each target query can be answered using at most b ≥ 1 feasible source queries, and that a Borel decoder maps every target output of error at most ε to a source output of error at most aε + c, where a, c ≥ 0. The simulator and the decoder are independent of the hidden source. Then 

T target  

> ε

≥ T source  

> aε +c

/b  .

Proof. Run the source algorithm with the seed of the target algorithm. Induction over the queries identifies the simulated transcript with the true target transcript. Decoding preserves the success event, and the simulation uses at most b times the target budget. The claim is the contrapositive of this implication between integer budgets. The same argument applies without change to a distributional source obstruction. We write QC p(d; m, r ; Q) for the certificate condition (76). The image condition implies that 

Q is onto and that ∥Q∥p→2 ≤ 1. A certificate is fixed before the source objective is chosen.

K.4 Uniform constant-distortion certificates 

We use the following form of Figiel et al. (1977, Theorem 2.6, Lemma 2.7 and equation (2.8)): let 

a|x|E ≤ ∥ x∥ ≤ b|x|E on an n-dimensional normed space, and let ME be the median of the norm on the E-unit sphere. For a universal η > 0 there is a subspace of dimension ⌊ηnM 2

/b 2⌋ whose Banach–Mazur distance from Euclidean space is at most 3/2. When b/a ≤ √n, the spherical mean and median are comparable up to universal factors. These results are classical; see also Milman and Schechtman (1986). The specialization below requires constants uniform in q, which Lemma K.8 provides. 67 Lemma K.8 (Spherical norm scale) . K.6 Smooth geometric transfer and its compatibility endpoint

Proof of Corollary K.1. Write Γ = d2/p −1. Norm comparison gives 

∥x∥2 ≤ ∥ x∥p ≤ √Γ∥x∥2, ∥u∥q ≤ ∥ u∥2.

Hence every Euclidean L-smooth function is L-smooth from ℓp to ℓq, and a Euclidean minimizer in Bd

> 2

(R/ √Γ) belongs to Bdp(R). Euclidean strong convexity with modulus μΓ implies (79) with 72 modulus μ. Applying Corollary 2.4 with radius R/ √Γ and Euclidean modulus μΓ gives (75) together with its exact accuracy window. The hard instances are globally defined quadratics, so the resulting lower bounds apply also to the corresponding larger ball-restricted smooth classes with feasible queries and output. If L ≥ μΓ and 0 < ε ≤ min {LR 2/(32Γ) , μR 2}, then (75) gives Ω( d min {d, pL/ (μΓ) }). This estimate is uniform at fixed μ throughout the displayed window. Sharp joint dependence on accuracy and conditioning for the general normed class remains open. 

Theorem K.16 (Compatibility and rigidity) . Let R > 0 and L, μ > 0. The class of convex functions differentiable on a neighborhood of Bdp(R), L-smooth from ℓp to ℓq on that ball, and 

μ-strongly convex there is nonempty if and only if 

L ≥ μΓ, Γ = d2/p −1.

At equality, every member agrees on the ball with f (x) = L∥x∥22/2 + b⊤x + c, so exact constrained optimization needs at most d + 1 feasible values. The global class Gp,μ has the same nonemptiness threshold and the same global quadratic form at equality; d + 1 unrestricted values suffice there as well. Proof. Choose h > 0 with hd 1/p < R and let σ be uniform on {− 1, 1}d. Strong convexity applied at opposite vertices gives 

Ef (hσ ) ≥ f (0) + μ

2 h2d2/p .

Smoothness implies the descent inequality along every feasible coordinate segment; in particular, 

[f (z + he i) + f (z − he i)] /2 ≤ f (z) + Lh 2/2. Reveal the signs one coordinate at a time. Partial vertices and connecting segments remain inside the ball, so iteration yields Ef (hσ ) ≤ f (0) + 

Ldh 2/2. Comparing the two bounds gives L ≥ μd 2/p −1. Conversely, L∥x∥22/2 has the required smoothness and strong-convexity modulus L/ Γ, and its global minimizer is zero. At equality, the gradient is locally Euclidean Lipschitz on the open ball. Its weak derivative is represented almost everywhere by a symmetric Hessian H. At common points of differentiability, the smoothness and strong-convexity inequalities give, for all v,

Hii ≤ L, v⊤Hv ≥ μ∥v∥2

> p

.

Averaging the second inequality over sign vectors gives tr H ≥ μd 2/p = Ld , so every diagonal entry equals L. Every quadratic form evaluated at a sign vector is at least Ld , and their average is Ld ; thus all of them equal Ld . For i̸ = j, multiplying by σiσj and averaging gives 2Hij = 0 .Therefore H = LI almost everywhere. The locally Lipschitz vector field ∇f − Lx has zero weak derivative. Mollifying on relatively compact interior balls makes its derivative identically zero, and passing to the limit gives local constancy. Connectedness then gives ∇f = Lx + b throughout the open ball. Integrating this continuous identity and extending to the boundary proves the formula. The identity is established on the open ball before integration, so no almost-everywhere statement is restricted to a line, which could be a null set. The same local argument on Rd

proves global rigidity. Query 0 and se i for i = 1 , . . . , d with a fixed 0 < s ≤ R. Then c = f (0) and bi =[f (se i) − f (0) − Ls 2/2] /s are known. Minimize the reconstructed quadratic over the known compact ball, or output −b/L in the global model. These computations require no further values. The estimate (75) is consistent with this endpoint: at L = μΓ the condition-number branch equals 1, so the bound reduces to 5d/ 16 , which matches the upper bound d + 1 in order. The rigidity theorem also rules out a lower bound growing logarithmically in the inverse accuracy uniformly up to the equality case. 73 L Independent norms: explicit profiles on ℓα balls 

This appendix proves Proposition I.1 and Corollary I.2 and their block refinements. Throughout, 

1 ≤ α, β ≤ ∞ are chosen independently, α∗ and β∗ denote the conjugate exponents, Bdα(Rα) is the closed ℓα ball of radius Rα, and θ, γ are defined in (55). All statements concern the image radius (5) and the lower bounds it yields. Section 6 identifies the matching complexity regimes.

Lemma L.3 (Range of the exponents) . We have 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 and θ + γ ≤ 1. Equality θ = 1 holds only for (α, β ) = (1 , ∞), and θ + γ = 1 only for (α, β ) ∈ { (1 , ∞), (∞, 1) }. Moreover, θ = 0 if and only if β ≤ 2 ≤ α, and θ > 1/2 forces γ = 0 .Proof. Each of the two summands of θ lies in [0 , 1/2] , and both vanish exactly when 1/β ≥ 1/2 ≥

1/α . As for θ + γ, if β ≥ α, then γ = 0 ; otherwise γ = 1 /β − 1/α , and there are three cases. If 

β ≤ 2 ≤ α, then θ = 0 and γ ≤ 1. If β < α ≤ 2, then θ = 1 /α − 1/2 and θ + γ = 1 /β − 1/2 ≤ 1/2.If 2 ≤ β < α , then θ = 1 /2 − 1/β and θ + γ = 1 /2 − 1/α ≤ 1/2. For the final claim, θ > 1/2

# M Nonsmooth hardness from sublevels of a known regularizer 

This appendix uses the external information theorem and the constants fixed in Proposition K.6; otherwise it is independent of the smooth proofs in Section J. Let B be the unit ball of a norm on Rd, let K be nonempty, compact and convex, and let the known continuous convex penalty

All policies are total Borel maps, the success probability is at least 2/3 on each objective, and a deterministic query budget applies to every history. Oracle and class boundaries. If the interface returns F at points outside K, the known penalty must have a specified finite extension there, so that its value can be added or subtracted. An interface that returns F only at feasible points needs no such extension. The result allows arbitrary known proximal operations for ψ, but it supplies no proximal oracle for the unknown g

or for F . The constant M bounds the Lipschitz modulus of g; requiring the full objective F to be Lipschitz would also require accounting for the variation of the regularizer, as in Theorem K.2. An affine tilt absorbed into g contributes to its Lipschitz constant. In the nonsmooth model, any such normalization must therefore preserve the prescribed budget M .

# N Upper–lower comparisons and their precise regimes 

We compare the lower bounds with known gradient-free algorithms to prove Corollaries 6.1 and 6.2. An exact scalar evaluation is the unit of cost: a two-point observation costs two queries. All lower bounds refer to success probability at least 2/3 and a deterministic worst-case query budget. Known randomized upper bounds stated in expected suboptimality are run to expected error at most ε/ 3; Markov’s inequality then supplies the same success probability without changing the order of a predetermined budget. 

N.1 Euclidean minimization 

On Qd,L,R , exact gradient differences and coefficient interpolation give O(d min {d, pLR 2/ε })

values (Section C; acceleration as in Bubeck (2015)). Theorem 2.1 supplies the matching lower bound for every 0 < ε ≤ LR 2/32 . The deterministic exact-recovery lower bound of Yao (1975) concerns the deterministic endpoint ε = 0 . Our bound applies to positive accuracy and randomized algorithms. For the full smooth class, finite-difference acceleration gives O(dpLR 2/ε ) values, and the cutting-plane construction in Section C.2 gives O(d2 log( LR 2/ε )) . Thus the best of these two documented upper bounds is

79 The polynomial lower bound matches the first branch when LR 2/d 2 ≤ ε ≤ LR 2/32 . At finer accuracy, Theorem E.5 gives a dimension-times-logarithm lower bound for the full smooth class. Determining its sharp joint dependence on dimension and accuracy remains open. For 0 < μ ≤ L, put κ = L/μ . On the globally μ-strongly convex subclass, Corollary 2.4 gives Ω( d min {d, √κ, pLR 2/ε }) for 0 < ε ≤ LR 2/32 . The accelerated method of Nesterov and Spokoiny (2017) gives O(d√κ log( LR 2/ε )) exact scalar values. A finite positive difference radius controls the approximation error; running to expected error ε/ 3 gives success probability 2/3

with a fixed budget. When κ ≤ d2 and ε ≤ μR 2 within this window, these polynomial orders match up to the accuracy logarithm. The smooth and nonsmooth ℓp strong-convexity bounds are given in Section O and Theorem K.2. The midpoint-curvature implementation in Theorem E.6 has the explicit budget

We use the mirror-descent bound here without invoking the extra parameter window needed to suppress the iteration term in an accelerated smoothing bound. 

N.4 Known upper bounds for arbitrary regularity norms

# O Strong-convexity compatibility in every coordinate norm 

The diagonal norm model requires both smoothness and strong convexity in ℓp, and localizes a global minimizer in Bdp(R). Corollary K.1 and Theorem K.16 treats 1 ≤ p ≤ 2. The complementary range gives the following unified statement. Here μ controls the strong convexity of the entire objective. 

Corollary O.1 (All coordinate norms) . Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and Γp = d|2/p −1|. The class of globally convex differentiable functions that are L-smooth and μ-strongly convex in ℓp, μ > 0, is nonempty precisely when L ≥ μΓp. Under this condition, localization in Bdp(R) implies the exact-value lower bound

At equality L = μΓp, every objective is a known quadratic curvature plus an unknown affine term: its Hessian is LI for p ≤ 2, and μI for p ≥ 2. Hence d + 1 values recover its minimizer exactly. Proof. For p ≤ 2, this is Corollary K.1 and Theorem K.16. Let p ≥ 2 and q = p/ (p − 1) .Euclidean μ-strongly convex quadratics of smoothness L/ Γp are admissible, because 

∥x∥p ≤ ∥ x∥2 ≤ d1/2−1/p ∥x∥p, ∥v∥q ≤ d1/2−1/p ∥v∥2.
