(R) lies in Bdp(R). Apply Corollary 2.4 with Euclidean smoothness L/ Γp. The oracle and decoder are unchanged, so the budget, randomization, and unrestricted query convention are preserved. For necessity, first take a smooth objective and write H = ∇2f (x). Strong convexity on coordinate directions gives Hii ≥ μ. For every sign vector s ∈ {− 1, 1}d, smoothness gives 

s⊤Hs ≤ Ld 2/p . Averaging over signs yields 

μd ≤ tr H ≤ Ld 2/p ,

which is equivalent to L ≥ μΓp. At equality all diagonal entries are μ, and every sign-vector quadratic form attains its common upper bound. Averaging s⊤Hs against sisj for i̸ = j forces 

Hij = 0 . Thus H = μI . For a globally C1,1 objective, convolution with a smooth compactly supported kernel preserves both norm inequalities. The preceding argument applies to each convolution; locally uniform convergence of gradients gives ∇f (x) = μx + b in the equality case. This also justifies necessity without a C2 assumption. Conversely, f (x) = μ∥x∥22/2 has the required strong convexity and smoothness μΓp when 

p ≥ 2. The analogous quadratic in Theorem K.16 supplies sufficiency for p ≤ 2. At equality write 

f (x) = h∥x∥22/2 + b⊤x + c with known h > 0. Then c = f (0) and bi = f (ei) − f (0) − h/ 2, which recovers −b/h from d + 1 values. The recovered minimizer satisfies the prescribed localization assumption. The threshold characterizes compatibility of smoothness and strong convexity in the objective class itself. At p = 2 both endpoint formulas coincide. Sharp complexity in the ill-conditioned regime remains open. 

# P A fixed reference function and relative smoothness 

We consider a fixed non-Euclidean reference function as part of the objective class. Fix an open convex domain Ω ⊂ Rd, a point c ∈ Ω, and a known convex C2 function h : Ω → R. Write 

Dh(x, c ) = h(x) − h(c) − ⟨∇ h(c), x − c⟩.

Following the relative-smoothness convention of Lu et al. (2016), require Lh − f to be convex on Ω. Also require a minimizer x⋆ ∈ Ω with Dh(x⋆, c ) ≤ ∆. Queries and the output may be arbitrary points of Ω; computations involving the fixed known h are free. The unknown function is accessed only through its exact values.

Global curvature and the fixed reference. The Hessian lower bound in (99) holds through-out the query domain, ensuring that every queried point satisfies the relative-smoothness condition. The reference function is fixed and known; the hard objectives vary within the resulting class. Sharp complexity for general fixed references remains open. A proximal oracle for the unknown objective returns an exact minimizer of f (x) + t−1Dh(x, c ).When this minimizer exists, comparison with x⋆ gives objective error at most ∆/t , so one such stronger oracle call with t ≥ ∆/ε is sufficient. This stronger information model therefore has a one-call guarantee for the same accuracy criterion. 

# Q Comparison of oracle models and proof dependencies 

We compare the function classes, query responses and regions, randomization, error criteria, and dimension ranges of the related oracle models in Table 6. 

Exact quadratic recovery. Yao (1975) addresses a question posed by C. W. Gear: how many adaptive evaluations of an unknown quadratic form in d variables are needed to determine its minimizer? The resulting worst-case lower bound is of order d2, while (d + 1)( d + 2) /2

evaluations suffice by interpolation; Protasov (1996) later gave an O(d2 log 2 d) value-only method for approximate minimization on this class. That setting concerns exact recovery by adaptive evaluation, with no accuracy parameter and no randomization. Corollary 2.2 recovers the same order in the regime pLR 2/ε ≥ d and extends the analysis in two respects. First, it treats positive accuracy ε through the profile d min {d, pLR 2/ε }, which interpolates between the quadratic dimension scale d2 and the linear dimension scale d. Second, it covers randomized algorithms with success probability 2/3. The worst-case guarantee is obtained from a fixed prior and its adaptive posterior, with the explicit constant 1/4096 .

First-order lower bounds. The local-oracle theory of Nemirovski and Yudin (1983), the non-Euclidean smooth reductions of Guzmán and Nemirovski (2015), and the randomized lower bounds of Diakonikolas and Guzmán (2020); Woodworth and Srebro (2017) motivate the accuracy scales. Comparisons with scalar measurements must account for the oracle responses and admissible dimension regimes of those results. Simulating a gradient with O(d) values transfers a first-order upper bound to scalar access. To quantify the unavoidable scalar cost, our argument instead controls the information gained from each exact value. 85 Table 6: Model distinctions relevant to the smooth product theorem. Each row specifies the information model and class used by the cited result. Work Information / class Relation to the present theorem Yao (1975) Exact values; exact minimizer of a quadratic form Classical Ω( d2) bound at the ε = 0 

exact-recovery endpoint; Corollary 2.2 treats positive accuracy. The original model has no accuracy parameter and no randomization. Simchowitz (2018) Gradient queries; random smooth quadratics Randomized matrix-based hardness for vector responses; our theorem quantifies the cost of scalar responses. Zhang et al. (2026) Exact values; randomized nonsmooth convex Supplies the Euclidean nonsmooth input to our geometric transfer; Theorem 2.1 is independent of this input. Kerger (2026) Exact values; deterministic nonsmooth convex Deterministic high-accuracy endpoint: 

Ω( d2/ log( d + 1)) at the single level 

ε ≍ d−1/2, with a near-matching upper bound at that deterministic endpoint. Yu et al. (2023, 2024) Noisy values; quadratic / strongly convex Sample-complexity lower bounds with noise; noiseless hardness requires a separate information argument. Theorem 2.1 Exact values; randomized smooth convex Unrestricted adaptive queries; a product bound without logarithmic loss, even for quadratic objectives. Section I Exact values; smooth 

and nonsmooth, any regularity norm and any feasible ball A common Euclidean-image radius determines both transfers; explicit ℓα/ℓ β

rates (Table 4) and the almost-Euclidean quotient branch on ℓp balls (Corollary I.4). 

Noise and smoothing. Gradient-free algorithms for smooth problems (Nesterov and Spokoiny, 2017) and accelerated stochastic derivative-free methods (Gorbunov et al., 2022) provide relevant upper-bound context. Two-point feedback (Duchi et al., 2015; Shamir, 2017) and black-box smoothing (Gasnikov et al., 2022) have their own sampling and domain conventions. Analytic smoothing (Nesterov, 2005) and infimal convolution (Moreau, 1965; Rockafellar and Wets, 1998) can change what an exact value reveals. We prove the smooth lower bound directly on quadratics, for which one exact value corresponds to one rank-one matrix measurement. Inexact first-order oracle analysis (Devolder et al., 2014) provides the approximation guarantees used in the upper-bound implementations. 

Non-Euclidean exact-value complexity. Duchi et al. (2015) establish norm-dependent minimax lower bounds in a stochastic observation model. Their oracle returns values of random sample functions, and their lower-bound constructions compare the resulting observation laws. Here each query returns the exact value of a fixed objective. In this model, Zhang et al. (2026) prove the Euclidean nonsmooth information bound that we transfer to non-Euclidean feasible sets. Corollaries 6.1 and 6.2 identify the matching non-Euclidean dimension–accuracy rates under Euclidean regularity. 

Geometric information. The Euclidean-image condition records the operator norm, image dimension, and inscribed radius separately. Theorem 3.1 lets the norm that controls smoothness and the compact set that contains a minimizer vary independently; translated image balls also 86 accommodate asymmetric sets. Theorem 3.2 applies the same scalar information theorem to actual sublevels of a fixed known regularizer, and its spectral gain compares two lower-bound formulas for the same known-composite class. Geometric reductions and Schatten examples have classical precedents (Guzmán and Nemirovski, 2015). That useful images exist follows from classical finite-dimensional Banach-space geometry (Figiel et al., 1977; Milman and Schechtman, 1986; Pisier, 1989), and the elementary identity image remains available when a constant-radius image has too few dimensions. Information-theoretic local-oracle work (Braun et al., 2017, 2024) provides further context. The nonsmooth numerical bound used here is the explicitly stated input from Zhang et al. (2026). 

External inputs to the smooth proof. Cordero-Erausquin (2019) supplies the whole-space matrix-weighted variance framework; we prove the bounded-domain version with its boundary term. We apply the Gaussian norm and pseudoinverse bounds of Halko et al. (2011). The other inputs are standard: Neumann existence and regularity, Gaussian regression, and Gamma integrals. These inputs suffice for the smooth proof. The nonsmooth transfer additionally uses the theorem of Zhang et al. (2026). 

# R Open problems 

The results lead to the following questions, extending the discussion in Section 7. • High accuracy and strong convexity for general smooth functions. Corollary 2.3 settles the range LR 2/d 2 ≤ ε ≤ LR 2/32 . Below this range the quadratic class has complex-ity Θ( d2) by exact reconstruction, whereas the general-class upper bound O(d2 log( LR 2/ε )) 

of Section C.2 grows with accuracy. Theorem E.5 establishes dimension-times-logarithm growth for each fixed 0 ≤ μ/L < 1, and Theorem 2.5 gives a uniform matching estimate for 

μ/L ∈ [1 /2, 1] . Taking the maximum with Corollary 2.4 yields a lower bound for growing condition numbers. What is the sharp joint dependence on dimension, conditioning, and accuracy in this regime? For general composite objectives, the fine-accuracy behavior beyond the saturation of (11) is likewise open. • Smaller image dimensions and the certificate envelope. Proposition J.8 determines the full-dimensional radius exactly, as Rαd−θ(α,β ) for every α, β . What is the complete profile Rm(Bdβ , Bdα(Rα)) for m < d ? The block and Euclidean-section certificates give complementary lower bounds, and the Gaussian estimate controls the ℓ1 endpoint. The choice of dimension raises a further question: the nonsmooth profile changes regime at 

γ + θ = 1 /2 (Corollary I.2), whereas the quotient construction lowers the dimension to retain a constant radius and a wider accuracy window. Beyond the Euclidean case, the resulting envelope over dimensions and certificates, and its relation to minimax complexity, are only partially understood. • Coupling on compact domains and unequal block dimensions. The compact bilinear reduction yields a square-root dependence on the inverse accuracy, whereas relevant upper bounds can have an inverse-linear dependence. Settling the sharp accuracy dependence requires a stronger construction or a better algorithm. Our coupled families use r =min {dx, d y}. The sharp dependence on both dimensions when the blocks differ greatly remains to be determined. The global-gap coupling bounds and their versions for local accuracy windows also leave open whether a logarithmic accuracy factor is necessary at fixed parameters, subject to quadratic reconstruction. • Mixed partial-gradient and scalar-value budgets. The mixed-oracle constructions bound scalar-value queries under unrestricted access to the known block’s gradient. What is the attainable region of scalar-value and partial-gradient query budgets? Its dependence on 87 whether the coupling is known is especially important: partial gradients can reveal a hidden coupling matrix directly, whereas a fixed known coupling can make the outer solution depend on the unknown inner solution without making the partial-gradient responses informative about that solution. • Sharp constants and alternative priors. How much of Theorem A.1 survives for non-Wishart priors, and what replaces 4096 ?

The first compares the two value-only procedures behind the quadratic upper bound; the second evaluates the analytic inequality and the trace identity on which the lower bound rests. Both checks use synthetic instances generated by the procedures below. The lower-bound hard prior is analyzed in Section B.

The following subsections give the full protocols and supplementary checks. 88 S.2 The two value-only procedures 

Instance generation. For each d ∈ { 16 , 32 , 64 }, each seed s ∈ { 0, . . . , 19 } and each family, we initialize NumPy’s default_rng with SeedSequence([20260921, d,s,j]) , where j = 0 for the singular family and j = 1 for the strongly convex contrast. A square matrix with independent standard Gaussian entries is orthogonalized by QR, with the signs fixed so that the diagonal of the triangular factor is positive; the resulting orthogonal matrix U is Haar-distributed on O(d).We set H = U diag( ξ1, . . . , ξ d)U ⊤ with singular family: ξ1 = 0 , ξi = 10 −6+6( i−2) /(d−2) (i ≥ 2) ,

strongly convex contrast: ξi = 10 −4+4( i−1) /(d−1) .

An independent Gaussian vector, projected off ker H in the singular family, is normalized to give x⋆

with ∥x⋆∥2 = 1 and Hx ⋆ ∈ range H. The oracle returns q(x) = 12 x⊤Hx − (Hx ⋆)⊤x + 12 (x⋆)⊤Hx ⋆.Hence L = R = 1 in both families, and the contrast has the prescribed condition number 10 4.The singular family has effective condition number 10 6 on range H, the invariant subspace that contains its iterates. The additive constant is kept so that the oracle does not silently reveal the minimum value. All computations use double precision. 

Scalar access and query accounting. Each procedure receives a callable exact-value oracle and the known parameters d, L . The oracle may evaluate a batch of points and charges one scalar query per row; it returns neither gradients nor coefficients. Reconstruction acquires the values at 0, ±ei, and ei + ej for i < j , exactly (d + 1)( d + 2) /2 queries, and then computes the minimum-norm minimizer from the recovered system — a least-squares solve, since H is singular here. Solving that system has no oracle cost in the model of the paper. Acceleration starts from x0 = y0 = 0 and t0 = 1 and, for k = 0 , . . . , 3071 , computes 

(gk)i = q(yk + ei) − q(yk − ei)2 , xk+1 = yk − gk/L, tk+1 = 1 + 

q

1 + 4 t2

> k

2 , yk+1 = xk+1 + tk − 1

tk+1 

(xk+1 − xk).

The central-difference step equals one. Over the reals, the identity gk = Hy k − Hx ⋆ is exact for quadratic objectives at any nonzero step, so an arbitrarily small step would add cancellation without any gain. Each iteration costs 2d scalar queries, and neither the condition number nor the true minimizer is passed to the solver. Every trajectory runs for the same fixed budget of 3072 iterations. A separate evaluator then computes the objective gap and identifies the first iterate that meets each of 15 targets, logarithmically spaced from 10 −1 to 10 −8. Target crossings are evaluated after the fixed-budget run. All 120 acceleration trajectories reach every target within that budget. The figure reports medians and interquartile ranges over the 20 seeds; the bands show the middle 50% of the observed instance-wise query counts. 

Measured query counts. Table 7 lists selected medians for the singular family. Fitting log 10 

of the median accelerated cost against log 10 (1 /ε ) over ε ≤ 10 −4 gives slopes 0.449 , 0.463 and 

0.466 at d = 16 , 32 , 64 , compared with the exponent 1/2 of the accuracy-dependent branch. The contrast-family slopes are 0.299 , 0.310 and 0.322 . The fitted slopes summarize the stated finite accuracy range for the two positive spectra. Indeed, adding 10 −6vv ⊤ for a unit null vector v

makes the first family globally strongly convex without changing its iterates or gaps, since every iterate is orthogonal to v; the additional terms also cancel in each central difference. Singularity therefore does not explain the difference between the two families. The recurrence uses neither a strong-convexity parameter nor a restart schedule. 89 Table 7: Median scalar queries for value-only acceleration on the singular family. The final column is the fixed reconstruction cost, which suffices for every listed target.

Cheaper updates and the dimension factor. A natural question is whether value-only methods that spend fewer than 2d values per update avoid the dimension factor. Figure 4 reports a separate comparison on the singular and conditioned families (30 new seeds per dimension, 

d ∈ { 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 }, L = R = 1 ) and on the isotropic family H = I with a unit-norm minimizer. Five methods receive the same instance and the same budget of 2d · 2048 values: the accelerated recurrence of the main experiment; gradient descent with the full central-difference gradient ( 2d

values per update, step 1/L ); random-coordinate descent (a uniformly random coordinate ei,the central difference at ±ei, two values per update, step 1/L ); random-direction descent (a uniformly random unit direction u, the central difference at ±u, two values per update, step 1/L 

along u without a dimension factor); and coefficient reconstruction. All finite differences use step one, which is exact for quadratics, and first hits are assessed after the run by the hidden evaluator, as in the main experiment; missed targets are right-censored at the budget and enter the medians as +∞.Three observations follow. First, on the singular and conditioned families, at every accuracy 

ε ≤ 10 −3 at which the three non-accelerated methods finish within the budget, the ratio of the median count of either two-value method to that of full finite-difference gradient descent lies in [0 .89 , 1.27] : the cheaper update is paid for by a proportionally larger number of updates, so the total number of values grows with d in the same way (Figure 4b). On the same cells the accelerated method needs 2 to 11 times fewer values than gradient descent, and reconstruction becomes the cheapest procedure at fine accuracy, as in Figure 3. Second, on the isotropic family every full-gradient method finishes in one update, that is, 2d values, whereas at ε = 10 −6 random-coordinate descent needs between 6d and 11 d values and random-direction descent between 

24 d and 26 d values (Figure 4c). Third, within the common budget the two-value methods and gradient descent reach ε = 3 × 10 −5 on the singular family at d = 64 in every run but miss 10 −5 in about a third of the runs, whereas acceleration reaches 3 × 10 −8 in every run. These comparisons quantify the query costs of the five algorithms on the specified finite instance families. 

Numerical error. Reconstruction attains objective error below 10 −12 on every instance. The largest entrywise error in the recovered Hessian is below 2 × 10 −16 , and in the recovered linear term below 3 × 10 −17 , that is, a few units in the last place. These magnitudes depend on the linear algebra library and on the platform. Per-iteration and reconstruction query costs are exact integers, whereas the first iterate that crosses a target can depend on floating-point rounding. The tolerance-free statements are the rational-arithmetic checks below. 

Exact arithmetic checks. A separate test uses Python’s rational Fraction arithmetic on 100 quadratics with rational coefficients: dimensions 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, each with 20 seeds. It verifies 1220 recovered coefficients against their source values, 380 central-difference gradient coordinates at random rational points, and 100 query counts against (d + 1)( d + 2) /2, each by exact equality. Such finite tests detect implementation mistakes; the general identities are proved in Section C. 90 10 7

S.3 The inequalities behind the lower bound 

The script scripts/verify_affine_slice.py evaluates Theorem A.1 and the trace identity of Lemma B.5 in dimension two, where an affine slice of Sym 2 of codimension r is a (3 − r)-dimensional domain and the conditional moments are computed by quadrature. For each 

ζ ∈ { 3/2, 5/2, 7/2, 9/2, 13 /2, 19 /2} it draws six slices of codimension one and six of codimension two, each cut by rank-one measurements ⟨xix⊤ 

> i

, W ⟩ with Gaussian xi through W = nI , and integrates ρ(W ) ∝ det( W )ζ e− tr W/ 2 over the slice by a composite midpoint rule on a fixed box of half-width 6n + 40 in tangent coordinates, with 1400 2 nodes in codimension one and 40000 nodes in codimension two. The outer-shell mass is recorded as a truncation diagnostic; no adaptive widening or resolution refinement is performed. Across the 72 slices, the ratio of λmax (M 1/2QM 1/2) to 1+( d+1) /(2 ζ) lies in [0 .99989 , 1.00009] 

on every codimension-one slice and in [0 .68 , 0.96] on every codimension-two slice. On the first family the maximum absolute and relative discrepancies are below 2.2 × 10 −4 and 1.1 × 10 −4,respectively. The identity tr( M J ) = nd − 2r holds with relative error at most 7 × 10 −5, attained at the smallest ζ, and below 10 −9 for ζ ≥ 9/2. These residuals measure agreement at the specified quadrature resolution. The conditional-slice identity is proved in Section B.

Higher-dimensional and adaptive fibers. A second run evaluates the same two identities on the realized fibers of adaptive histories in d ∈ { 2, 3, 5} with ζ = 9 /2, that is, with n = d + 10 ,under three query policies: independent random unit directions, one unit direction repeated (so that the rank stays one), and a history-dependent rule in which each new unit direction is computed from the answers received so far. For each policy and each of 12 seeds, the conditional moments are recorded after q ∈ { 1, d, 2d} queries for d ∈ { 3, 5} and after q ∈ { 1, 3} queries for 

d = 2 . On a fiber of rank r the conditional law is an integral over d(d + 1) /2 − r dimensions. For d = 2 it is evaluated by quadrature on three nested grids (128, 256 and 512 nodes per axis on boxes of half-width 6n, 10 n and 16 n in tangent coordinates); for d = 3 , 5 it is sampled by slice sampling with four chains, 2000 warm-up sweeps and 8192 retained draws thinned by two; a full-rank fiber is a point mass and is evaluated exactly. Table 8 summarizes the results. On every rank-one fiber the ratio of λmax (M 1/2QM 1/2) to the bound 1 + ( d + 1) /(2 ζ) equals one up to quadrature error in d = 2 and lies in [0 .994 , 1.026] in d = 3 , 5, consistent with equality on rank-one fibers up to a Monte Carlo error of a few percent; on fibers of higher rank the ratio decreases, down to the point-mass value 2ζ/ (2 ζ + d + 1) at full rank. The trace identity holds with relative residual below 1.1 × 10 −6 under quadrature and below 1.1 × 10 −2 under sampling. The operator norm of the posterior inverse J, averaged over the twelve histories of a policy, lies between 1.09 and 1.98 , whereas the budget ϕ(2 Er/n ) of Proposition B.7, evaluated at the empirical mean rank, lies between 1.67 and 4.44 ; the budget therefore holds with a wide margin in all 24 policy–query cells. These are numerical residuals at the stated grid and sampler settings. The exact disintegration and moment identities are established in Section B. 

Saddle and geometry diagnostics. The script scripts/verify_saddle_algebra.py per-forms 4284 numerical checks over four seeds and the dimension pairs (1 , 8) , (8 , 1) , (3 , 7) , (7 , 3) , (5 , 5) .They cover the two- and five-value payoff identities, stationarity, rectangular padding, global and compact gap transfers, compact response windows, the known-diagonal bias bound, the algebraic identities behind the mixed-oracle reductions, and coefficient reconstruction. The shared-curvature and known-diagonal gaps are compared with independently computed best responses, and the compact bilinear gap is checked against its closed form, derived in Theorem G.1. 92 10 8

Two value-only policies for known diagonal curvatures. Figure 5 illustrates Propo-sition G.10 on strongly convex–strongly concave quadratics with known diagonal Hessians 

μx = μy = 1 , block pairs (dx, d y) ∈ { (8 , 32) , (32 , 8) , (16 , 16) , (16 , 64) , (64 , 16) , (32 , 32) }, coupling operator norm 1 or 10 , and ten seeds per pair. Two value-only policies receive the same instances and the same budget of 4( dx + dy) values per iteration for 512 iterations: an extragradient method whose two partial gradients are recovered exactly by central differences at each of its two points, and the reconstruction of Proposition G.10, which uses 1 + dx + dy + dxdy values and then solves the stationarity equations. The error is the global gap, evaluated in closed form from the two partial gradients and checked against independently computed best responses (largest discrepancy 

8.9 × 10 −16 ); the evaluator never influences the updates. Reconstruction returns the saddle point at its fixed cost, independently of the coupling and of the accuracy, with gap at most 4.5 × 10 −29 

and coefficient errors at most 1.2 × 10 −15 . The extragradient method reaches every target down to 10 −8 within the budget, but its cost grows with the accuracy and with the coupling: raising the coupling norm from 1 to 10 multiplies its median cost by a factor between 4.5 and 5.3 at every pair and target, whereas the reconstruction cost is unchanged. The experiment compares the two procedures on the stated strongly convex–strongly concave quadratic family. A separate run checks the two recovery counts of Propositions G.2 and G.3 on bilinear-affine instances with a global saddle point (x⋆, 0) , ∥x⋆∥2 ≤ 1, for the block pairs (d, 1) and (1 , d ) with 

d ∈ { 8, 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 , 256 } and for (8 , 8) , (8 , 32) , (32 , 8) , with two instance families and twenty seeds per pair. In all 600 runs, coefficient recovery used exactly dy(dx + 1) values and compressed recovery exactly dx(dx + 1) values, and both returned pairs with zero compact gap up to rounding; for dx = 1 the compressed rule needs two values whatever dy is, as noted after Proposition G.3. 93 T Comparison with the concurrent work of Wu et al. 

We compare the smooth exact-value guarantees of Wu et al. (2026) with ours. We write L for their Euclidean smoothness constant β and express both results in the notation of this paper.

Their lower bound assumes d ≥ d0 and 0 < ε ≤ cεLR 2 for universal constants. Their Theorems 3.3–3.5 and Corollary 3.6 match the upper and lower orders when 

LR 2(log( ed )/d )2/3 ≤ ε ≤ cεLR 2.

Our full-class matching range is LR 2/d 2 ≤ ε ≤ LR 2/32 (Corollary 2.3). Our quadratic matching result additionally covers every smaller positive accuracy in the stated window (Corollary 2.2). To compare the same localization promise, substitute R/ 2 for our radius. This changes only absolute constants in these orders. The two models retain their respective query regions. 

T.2 Hard instances and query access 

Their construction uses Moreau smoothing, exact prefix shielding, and delayed rotations. The bounded-query norm enters its spherical-cap step. Our Wishart argument instead controls the conditional law on every realized affine slice, under a fixed prior independent of the algorithmic seed. This conditional-law argument permits randomization and arbitrary finite query norms, including points outside the localization ball. Their upper guarantee keeps all queries in the prescribed ball. Our finite-difference implemen-tation in Section C.2 allows queries outside the localization set. For quadratics, coefficient recovery matches the d2 plateau. For the full smooth class, the nonquadratic multiscale construction gives the additional accuracy dependence in Theorem 2.5. 

T.3 Non-Euclidean and saddle-point extensions 

Theorems 3.1 to 3.3 extend the exact-value analysis to independent regularity and localization geometries and actual sublevels of a known regularizer. The nonsmooth transfer uses the Euclidean information theorem of Zhang et al. (2026). Theorem 2.5 gives a uniform matching estimate near μ = L. The saddle-point results cover unequal blocks and separate compact and global gaps, as well as scalar and mixed access. Section 6 identifies matching non-Euclidean regimes, and Section R gives the remaining questions for general geometries, coupling, and growing condition numbers. 94
